- **Shared-implementation design, mirroring Phase 6B's runtime-detector extraction but scoped smaller.** Unlike Phase 6B (where the shared logic moved to `Infrastructure` because Windows also needed it), Docker/Podman sharing is Linux-internal — `ContainerCliRuntimeProvider` stays `internal` to `ServerSleuth.Linux.Containers`, with `DockerContainerRuntimeProvider`/`PodmanContainerRuntimeProvider` as its only public surface. No Windows container discovery exists yet, so there was nothing to extract further upward.
- **Runtime detection never trusts PATH presence alone.** `ContainerRuntimeAvailability` is derived only from actually invoking the runtime: a process-start failure is `NotInstalled`; a non-zero exit whose stderr contains "permission denied" (verified against both Docker's and Podman's own phrasing) is `AccessDenied`; any other failure is `Unavailable`. A `docker` executable merely existing on PATH is never classified as "Docker supported."
- **Two-step bulk-inspect pattern, improved over Phase 6A's systemd approach.** `systemctl list-units` + `show` (Phase 6A) queries one unit at a time; here, `docker inspect id1 id2 id3 ...` returns a single JSON array covering every container/network in one call, avoiding an N+1 command pattern while still remaining a single fixed, read-only subcommand.
- **Environment variable names only, never values — the strictest secret boundary in this codebase so far.** Every other scanner that touches secrets (Cron, Configuration) redacts values found in already-serialized text; here, the raw value is discarded before it ever reaches an entity, not merely masked, since container environment variables are disproportionately likely to carry live credentials directly (`DB_PASSWORD=...`, `API_KEY=...`).
- **Identity is always runtime-qualified**: `container:{runtime}:{id}`, `image:{runtime}:{id}`, `volume:{runtime}:{name}`, `network:{runtime}:{id}` — a Docker and a Podman entity that coincidentally share an ID (both use 64-hex-character IDs from the same underlying container-runtime lineage) must never collide into one entity.
- **Deliberately no `Container → host Process` correlation in this phase.** Port mappings and container PIDs are captured as facts on the `Container` entity, but the scanner never asserts a relationship to a host `Process` entity from PID, command line, or port number alone — container networking and host socket ownership are different evidence domains, and namespace-based correlation (if ever added) belongs to Phase 5 analysis, not scanner-time inference.
- **No raw `docker inspect` JSON is ever persisted** — every field consumed from the DTOs (`DockerJsonDtos.cs`) is copied into a specific, named entity/metadata field; anything not explicitly mapped is discarded, not carried forward as an opaque blob.
- **Known, disclosed scope boundary**: Image `OS`/`Architecture` are left `null` (not guessed) since `docker images --format json` doesn't expose them without a second per-image `inspect` call, which was judged out of scope for this phase's command budget.

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 6C entry and `SCANNERS.md`'s Linux Container Scanner section.

## Phase 6D Addendum — Linux Kubernetes Discovery (as built)

One new scanner in `src/ServerSleuth.Linux/Kubernetes/`, plus the first genuinely new Core entity models since Phase 1.

```
LinuxKubernetesScanner
   ← IKubernetesProvider (KubectlKubernetesProvider — the only implementation; unlike Phase
      6C's Docker/Podman split, there is only one Kubernetes acquisition path, so the interface
      exists for a future non-kubectl path, not to switch between two current ones)
   → Cluster | KubernetesNamespace | KubernetesNode | KubernetesPod | Container
     (Type="KubernetesContainer") | KubernetesWorkload | KubernetesService |
     KubernetesIngress | KubernetesConfigMap | KubernetesSecret |
     KubernetesPersistentVolumeClaim | KubernetesPersistentVolume
```

- **First new Core models since Phase 1.** Every Linux phase through 6C reused existing Phase 1 entities (`Package`, `Runtime`/`Sdk`, `ScheduledTask`, `Container`). Kubernetes' resource shapes (Namespace/Node/Pod/Workload/Service/Ingress/ConfigMap/Secret/PVC/PV) have no honest analog among them, so ten new `DiscoveryEntity` subclasses were added — still following the instruction to prefer these specific names over inventing provider-specific ones (no `KubectlDeployment`, etc.). Pod containers are the one exception that *does* reuse an existing model: `Container` (Type="KubernetesContainer"), since a container-within-a-pod is semantically close enough to the Phase 6C container shape to justify reuse over a new type.
- **Single-provider design, deliberately not mirroring Phase 6C's two-branch pattern.** Docker/Podman needed two providers because two real runtimes exist; Kubernetes access has exactly one real acquisition path (`kubectl`, using its own already-configured context), so `IKubernetesProvider` has one implementation. The interface still exists (rather than the scanner calling `KubectlKubernetesProvider` directly) purely to keep the door open for a future non-CLI acquisition path without touching the scanner.
- **Availability requires an actual successful API query, never PATH presence.** `KubernetesAvailability` is derived from `kubectl version -o json`'s own outcome — a `kubectl` executable existing but unable to reach any cluster is `Unavailable`, never `Supported`. This mirrors the exact discipline Phase 6C established for `docker`/`podman` presence vs. reachability.
- **Secret values are structurally prevented from being materialized, not merely filtered afterward.** `SecretItem.Data` (the internal DTO parsing `kubectl get secrets -o json`) is typed `Dictionary<string, JsonElement>` specifically so the base64 payload is never converted into a usable C# string anywhere in the provider — only `.Keys` is read. This is a stronger guarantee than "redact after storing," matching the CRITICAL severity the phase's own instructions gave Secret handling (skill.md §15), and goes one step further than Phase 6C's "store then redact" approach for container labels/env values.
- **A real bug found via test failure, not assumed.** A malformed top-level JSON response for one resource kind was initially treated as "zero resources found, scan still fully Supported" (`TryDeserialize` returning `null`, and the caller not distinguishing that from a genuinely empty `items: []`). A dedicated test caught this; `GetList<T>` now records a partial failure whenever deserialization returns `null`, degrading the scanner to `PartiallySupported` rather than silently reporting a false-empty result as success.
- **Cluster identity is opportunistic, never invented.** `Cluster.ClusterIdentifier` is populated only from the `kube-system` namespace's own UID when the namespaces query happens to return it — no GUID is generated, and the field is simply `null` on the (rare) cluster lacking a `kube-system` namespace, per skill.md §6/§22's explicit "do not invent cluster IDs."
- **Deliberately no `KubernetesPod`/`KubernetesContainer` → host Process/Container correlation in this phase**, mirroring exactly the boundary Phase 6C drew for Docker/Podman host containers — that correlation, if ever added, belongs to Phase 5 analysis.
- **hostPath is a string field, never a filesystem operation.** `PvRow.HostPath`/`KubernetesPersistentVolume.HostPath` carry only the path string reported by the Kubernetes API for a remote cluster's node — this scanner performs no filesystem access of any kind for this field, trivially satisfying "never read, never recursively scan, never follow symlinks" (skill.md §18).

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 6D entry and `SCANNERS.md`'s Linux Kubernetes Scanner section.

## Phase 6E Addendum — Linux Configuration Discovery (as built)

The significant architectural event this phase was a cross-cutting extraction, not just a new Linux scanner — the first time a piece of `ServerSleuth.Windows` moved to `ServerSleuth.Infrastructure` specifically so Linux could reuse it (Phase 6B's `RuntimeEntityBuilder` extraction was the only precedent).

```
ServerSleuth.Infrastructure.Configuration (NEW — moved + extended from ServerSleuth.Windows.Configuration)
   ConfigurationFormat (+ EnvFile), ConfigurationParseStatus, ScanRoot, ConfigurationFileRow,
   ConfigurationAnalysisResult (+ NetworkStorageReferences, UnixSocketReferences),
   ExternalEndpointReference, DatabaseReference, UncPathReference, NetworkStorageReference (NEW),
   ConfigurationFormatDetector, ConfigurationContentAnalyzer (+ bare $VAR, ${VAR:-default},
   Unix socket, NFS, CIFS patterns), StructuralValidator, ScanRootCollector
   (Service/ScheduledTask-derived pieces only — IIS stays Windows-side)

LinuxConfigurationScanner (src/ServerSleuth.Linux/Configuration/)
   ← LinuxScanRootCollector (well-known technology dirs + Service/ScheduledTask app roots)
   ← LinuxConfigurationTechnologyAnalyzer (systemd/nginx/Apache/PHP/MySQL/PostgreSQL/SSH)
   → Core.Models.Configuration (Phase 1, unchanged)
```

- **What moved, and why it's genuinely platform-neutral.** Every type listed above is pure data/pure text-processing — none of it touches the registry, WMI, `ServiceController`, or any other Windows-only API. `ConfigurationContentAnalyzer`'s regexes operate on already-decoded text; `StructuralValidator` uses only `System.Text.Json`/`System.Xml` (BCL, cross-platform); `ScanRoot`/`ConfigurationFileRow`/`ConfigurationAnalysisResult` are plain records. The one piece of `ScanRootCollector` that stayed Windows-side — deriving roots from IIS `WebSite`/`Application` entities — is genuinely Windows-only (Linux has no IIS), so it remains a thin wrapper in `ServerSleuth.Windows.Configuration` that calls the shared `CollectFromServices`/`CollectFromScheduledTasks`/`Deduplicate` helpers for its other two-thirds.
- **Case sensitivity is a real, load-bearing platform difference, not just cosmetic.** The shared `ScanRootCollector.Deduplicate` now takes a `StringComparer` (and an optional normalization function) as parameters specifically so Windows can keep its `OrdinalIgnoreCase` + trailing-backslash-trim behavior byte-for-byte unchanged, while Linux passes `Ordinal` + trailing-forward-slash-trim — `/opt/ERP` and `/opt/erp` are two different, legitimate directories on Linux and must never collapse into one scan root.
- **Application-root derivation differs by one deliberate rule, not by accident.** Windows's `ScanRootCollector` uses a service's executable directory as-is. Linux's `LinuxScanRootCollector.DeriveApplicationRoot` adds exactly one additional step: if the executable's directory is named `bin` or `sbin` (the ubiquitous `/opt/app/bin/executable` convention that has no Windows analog), climb one level to the parent — but never climb if the parent would be `/` itself, and never climb for any other directory name. This is a genuine, documented, bounded difference between the two platforms' otherwise-identical derivation logic, not a missed generalization.
- **`/etc/cron.d` is deliberately never scanned as a Configuration root**, even though it appears in skill.md (Phase 6E) §4's own "well-known roots" list — its header note ("do not duplicate Cron job discovery responsibilities from Phase 6B") takes precedence, since Phase 6B's `LinuxScheduledTaskScanner` already produces `ScheduledTask` entities for exactly those files; scanning them again here would create a duplicate `Configuration` entity carrying no new information for the same file.
- **Per-technology parsers are additive metadata, never a replacement for the generic analyzer.** Every file — regardless of which technology's directory it came from — still runs through the full `ConfigurationContentAnalyzer` (secrets, endpoints, database references, environment references, network storage, Unix sockets). `LinuxConfigurationTechnologyAnalyzer` only adds *extra*, technology-specific facts (e.g. nginx's `listen`/`server_name`, systemd's `ExecStart`/`User`) on top, dispatched by `ScanRoot.Source` rather than by content-sniffing, so a file's technology classification is always traceable to which bounded root found it.
- **The systemd `EnvironmentFile=` second scan pass is bounded to depth one.** An `.env` file discovered this way is analyzed generically and attributed to the referencing unit's `Service` entity as owner, but is never itself inspected for further `EnvironmentFile=` references — there is no recursive expansion, matching the same "never expand indefinitely" discipline as every other bounded-root rule in this phase.
- **A real, pre-existing `SecretRedactor` gap was found and fixed, verified against the full pre-fix baseline.** `SecretRedactor`'s key-value rules anchored their leading boundary with a plain `\b`, which — because `_` is a word character in regex — never matched the underscore-prefixed key convention that is the *default* style in Linux `.env` files and systemd unit files (`DB_PASSWORD=...`, not `Password=...`). This is precisely the running example skill.md (Phase 6E) §9 uses to specify the expected behavior, so it surfaced immediately as a failing test rather than being missed. Fixed by changing the leading anchor from `\b` to `(?<![A-Za-z])` — rejects only an *immediately preceding letter* (so `MyPassword=...`-style camelCase run-ons remain correctly unmatched, exactly as before), while now accepting an immediately preceding underscore. The trailing `\b` is untouched, so `TokenType=...`/`SecretQuestion=...` prefix false positives remain correctly rejected. Verified against the complete pre-fix 758-test baseline (all five projects) with zero regressions before this fix was accepted.

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 6E entry and `SCANNERS.md`'s Linux Configuration Scanner section.

## Phase 6F Addendum — Linux Native Dependency Discovery (as built)

```
LinuxNativeDependencyScanner (src/ServerSleuth.Linux/Native/)
   ← already-discovered paths: Process.Path, Service.ExecutablePath, ScheduledTask.Action,
     Runtime.Path/Sdk.Path (re-runs LinuxProcessScanner/LinuxSystemdServiceScanner/
     LinuxScheduledTaskScanner/LinuxRuntimeDiscoveryScanner — never a filesystem ELF search)
   ← ILinuxElfParser → ElfParser (pure, no I/O — takes already-read bytes)
   ← ILibraryResolver → LinuxLibraryResolver (bounded, 5-tier resolution)
        ← ILdconfigProvider → LdconfigProvider (optional tier 5; `ldconfig -p` only)
   → Core.Models.Dll (Phase 1, unchanged; Type="NativeBinary")
```

- **Three-way separation, deliberately not one class.** ELF parsing (`ElfParser`), dependency resolution (`LinuxLibraryResolver`), and entity creation/orchestration (`LinuxNativeDependencyScanner`) are three separate types with no circular knowledge of each other — `ElfParser` doesn't know what a "library resolution tier" is, `LinuxLibraryResolver` doesn't know what an ELF dynamic section looks like. This mirrors the Windows PE/COM split (parsing vs. root collection vs. scanner) and was an explicit instruction this phase ("keep ELF parsing separate from dependency resolution... do not create a giant class").
- **A hand-written ELF parser, not a third-party library.** skill.md's own instruction ("implement the minimum ELF parser required") ruled out pulling in a full ELF-tooling package for what amounts to reading a handful of well-documented, stable binary structures (the ELF header, program header table, and dynamic section array) — all fixed-layout, well-specified formats with no version skew risk significant enough to justify a dependency. Verified correct against 19 tests built on a companion `SyntheticElfBuilder` test helper that constructs real, structurally valid ELF32/ELF64 byte arrays (identity virtual-address mapping, so DT_STRTAB's virtual address always translates via the same PT_LOAD-segment logic the parser itself uses) — never relying on a real machine binary as the only test source, per the phase's own instruction.
- **Big-endian ELF is a recognized dead end, not a crash.** The parser reads `EI_CLASS`/`EI_DATA` (both single bytes, endian-independent) and, on finding big-endian, stops immediately — every field after that point (`e_machine`, offsets, sizes) is multi-byte and would need explicit byte-swapping this parser doesn't implement. Returning `UnsupportedEndian` with a diagnostic, rather than either crashing or silently byte-swapping incorrectly, was the explicit instruction ("do not guess").
- **This phase resolves a limitation carried since Phase 4B/4D/6A**: "per-runtime architecture detection remains out of scope" is now false for Linux ELF binaries — `EntityArchitecture` gained `RiscV` (additive; every existing `switch` over the enum already has a `_ =>` default arm, so this was a zero-risk addition verified by the full regression), and the ELF `e_machine` → `EntityArchitecture` mapping covers x86/x86_64/ARM/AArch64/RISC-V explicitly, with an unrecognized value preserved as raw metadata rather than discarded.
- **Five-tier, fully ordered dependency resolution — Ambiguous has exactly one trigger.** RPATH and RUNPATH are inherently ordered (first match in file order wins, mirroring the real dynamic loader), and the well-known-locations list is a fixed, documented array (also ordered, first match wins) — none of these three tiers can produce `Ambiguous`. The *only* tier that can is "already-discovered binary paths sharing the exact filename": if two independently-discovered binaries happen to share a library's filename at two different full paths, there genuinely is no principled way to prefer one over the other, so that's the one case `Ambiguous` (with every candidate recorded) is returned.
- **`$ORIGIN` is expanded because it is bounded and deterministic; `$LIB`/`$PLATFORM` are not, because they aren't (for this scanner's purposes).** `$ORIGIN` always means exactly one thing — the importing binary's own directory, which this scanner already knows. `$LIB`/`$PLATFORM` depend on runtime CPU/ABI detection this scanner has no reason to perform (and skill.md's own architecture-detection instruction explicitly forbids inferring architecture from anything other than the ELF header itself) — left unexpanded and documented as a known limitation rather than guessed at.
- **Path normalization is pure string manipulation, never a filesystem call.** `NativePathNormalizer.Normalize` collapses `.`/`..` segments by popping a in-memory list — a `..` at the top of the stack is simply dropped rather than allowed to underflow past `/`. No `Path.GetFullPath`/`Directory.Exists` walk is ever used for this, so a maliciously crafted RPATH entry can never cause traversal outside the (conceptually unbounded, since Linux libraries can legitimately live almost anywhere, but always absolute and always root-relative) path space — the actual protection is that only ONE exact, normalized candidate path is ever existence-checked per tier/entry, never a directory enumeration.
- **`ldd` was deliberately never implemented**, exactly matching the phase's stated preference — DT_NEEDED parsing (already-implemented, authoritative, execution-free) fully covers dependency discovery without needing to execute the target binary through the dynamic loader the way `ldd` does.
- **Only `ldconfig -p` is ever invoked, verified structurally, not just behaviorally.** A dedicated negative-security test inspects every actual `FakeProcessRunner` invocation across a full discovery run and asserts the executable is always exactly `ldconfig` with arguments always exactly `["-p"]` — never a bare `ldconfig`, which could rebuild the cache.

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 6F entry and `SCANNERS.md`'s Linux Native Dependency Scanner section.

## Phase 6G Addendum — Cross-Platform Integration & Real Linux Validation (as built)

This phase's architectural contribution is small in code volume (one orchestration layer, one new test project) but large in what it proves — for the first time in this project's history, real code ran on a real Linux machine, and two genuine bugs were found this way that six prior phases of Windows-only Windows-authored-fixture testing never surfaced.

```
ServerSleuth.Core/Orchestration/ (NEW — platform-neutral, references neither Windows nor Linux)
   IDiscoveryScannerRegistry / DiscoveryScannerRegistry  (deterministic Id-sorted catalog)
   IDiscoveryEngine / DiscoveryEngine                    (runs every scanner, isolates failures)
   AggregateDiscoveryResult                              (entities/errors/statuses/diagnostics)

ServerSleuth.Integration.Tests (NEW project, net8.0-windows, references Windows AND Linux)
   CrossPlatformCompositionTests       — DI union proof
   CrossPlatformAnalysisFixtureTests   — mixed-platform entities through the Analysis pipeline
```

- **Why the orchestrator lives in Core, not a new "Orchestration" project.** `IDiscoveryScannerRegistry`/`DiscoveryEngine` only depend on `IDiscoveryScanner`/`DiscoveryContext`/`DiscoveryResult` — all already in Core. Creating a separate assembly for two small classes would have been pure ceremony; Core is already the one place both Windows and Linux (and now Integration.Tests) can reference without creating a new cross-project dependency edge.
- **Why `AddServerSleuthDiscoveryEngine()` is a separate DI method, not folded into `AddServerSleuthInfrastructure()`.** The registry's whole value is enumerating `IEnumerable<IDiscoveryScanner>` — which only has members after `AddServerSleuthWindows()`/`AddServerSleuthLinux()` have run. Registering the registry inside `AddServerSleuthInfrastructure()` (which every composition root calls *before* the platform methods, per the existing convention) would silently capture an empty scanner set at resolution time in some DI container configurations. Keeping it separate makes the ordering dependency explicit in the composition root's own code, not hidden inside a method name that doesn't suggest it.
- **Why a new `ServerSleuth.Integration.Tests` project, rather than adding these tests to an existing one.** No existing test project references both `ServerSleuth.Windows` and `ServerSleuth.Linux` — Windows.Tests and Linux.Tests are deliberately platform-scoped, and Analysis.Tests is deliberately platform-neutral (it constructs Core entities directly, never touching either platform assembly). Proving "compose both platforms together and get the union" genuinely requires a project that references both; inventing that in an existing project would have blurred each existing project's own architectural boundary.
- **Why the fixture in `CrossPlatformAnalysisFixtureTests` constructs entities directly rather than running real Windows/Linux scanners.** The phase's own goal (§17) is proving the *shared vocabulary* and the *Analysis pipeline's* behavior on mixed-platform data — not re-testing scanner discovery logic, which every prior phase's own test suite already covers exhaustively. Constructing entities directly keeps this test fast, deterministic, and focused on exactly what it's meant to prove.
- **A genuine WSL2 Ubuntu 24.04 LTS instance was found already present on this machine** (via `wsl --list`), rather than needing to be provisioned — exactly the distro skill.md (Phase 6G) §11 names as preferred. With the user's explicit, separately-obtained approval, the .NET 8 runtime and .NET 10 SDK (matching the Windows host's SDK version exactly, needed because `ServerSleuth.slnx` requires `.slnx`-aware tooling) were installed **user-locally** (`dotnet-install.sh --install-dir $HOME/.dotnet`, no `sudo`, no system-wide package manager changes) — this is build/test tooling, not a scan target, and is explicitly distinct from the "do not install Docker/Podman/Kubernetes merely to make the smoke test pass" instruction (§12), which was honored exactly: neither was installed, and the real smoke test run correctly reported Podman/Kubernetes as `NotInstalled` and Docker as `Unavailable` (CLI present via Windows/WSL interop, daemon integration not enabled for this distro) — real, unmodified facts about the environment as found.
- **Bug #1 — `System.IO.Path` host-OS-dependence in `ServerSleuth.Analysis`.** `BinaryImportsBinaryRule`/`ApplicationBoundaryEngine` called `System.IO.Path.GetDirectoryName`/`Path.Combine` directly on paths that `WindowsPathNormalizer.Normalize` had already canonicalized to backslash form. On Windows, `System.IO.Path` treats `\` as the separator, so this worked by construction. On Linux, `System.IO.Path` does not recognize `\` as a separator at all — `GetDirectoryName` on a string with no `/` in it returns an empty string or leaves the string unchanged depending on shape, silently breaking every directory-based correlation rule for Windows-sourced paths whenever Analysis happens to run on a Linux host. This is precisely the scenario a real "central analysis service ingesting both Windows-sourced and Linux-sourced discovery data" deployment would hit. Fixed by adding `WindowsPathNormalizer.GetDirectoryName`/`Combine`/`GetFileName` — hand-rolled, backslash-only, and therefore correct regardless of which OS is currently executing the code — mirroring the exact same class of fix Phase 6B applied to `LinuxPath` in the opposite direction (forward-slash paths misinterpreted by a Windows-running assembly). Five real Analysis test failures on Linux (all traceable to this one root cause) were resolved by this single fix, verified with zero regressions against the pre-fix 146-test Windows baseline both before and after.
- **Bug #2 — `ProcessRunner` status classification gap in `KubectlKubernetesProvider`/`ContainerCliRuntimeProvider`.** Both providers' `Classify` methods checked only `OperationStatus.StartFailed` to detect "executable not found," but the real `ProcessRunner` (per its own pre-existing, already-passing test) classifies a genuinely-missing executable as the more specific `OperationStatus.NotFound`. Every prior test of this classification path used `FakeProcessRunner`, whose documented default behavior for an unregistered command is to return `StartFailedResult` — meaning the `NotFound` code path was never exercised by any fixture, ever, until a real machine with `kubectl` genuinely absent hit it for the first time. Fixed by checking both statuses; a new fixture test for each provider now pins the exact `NotFound` status the real runner produces, so this gap can never silently regress even on a Windows-only CI run.
- **Both bugs were found by running EXISTING, unmodified test suites against a real OS — not by writing new exploratory tests first.** This is the methodological point of "real Linux validation": the bugs were latent in code that had passed 100% of its Windows-authored fixture coverage for multiple phases: the fixtures were simply never adversarial to the one assumption (host OS matches path style) that happened to be false in this environment.

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 6G entry and `SCANNERS.md`'s Cross-Platform Discovery Orchestration section.

## Phase 7A Addendum — Evidence-Based Risk Engine (as built)

The first Analysis layer that sits *after* Correlation/Boundary/Expansion/Validation rather than alongside them — it consumes their already-produced output and adds nothing to the discovery/correlation surface.

```
ServerSleuth.Analysis/Risk/ (NEW — references only ServerSleuth.Core, exactly like the rest of Analysis)
   Models/
      RiskFinding            (deterministic Id — see below; Category/Severity/Confidence/Evidence/Recommendation)
      RiskCategory            (closed 12-value taxonomy)
      RiskSeverity             (Critical/High/Medium/Low/Info — no numeric score)
      RiskAnalysisResult      (Findings + Diagnostics)
   RiskAnalysisContext        (immutable, pre-indexed view over Discovery/Correlation/Boundary/Expansion/Validation output)
   Rules/
      IRiskRule                (Id, Category, DefaultSeverity, Evaluate(RiskAnalysisContext))
      RR1-RR12                 (MissingDependency, MissingBinary, AccessDenied, MissingRuntime,
                                 CertificateExpiry, ServiceDependency, ScheduledTaskDependency,
                                 ComDependency, ExternalDependency, SharedInfrastructure,
                                 ConfigurationRisk, GraphIntegrity)
   Engine/RiskRuleEngine        (deterministic Id-ordered execution, failure isolation, evidence
                                 invariant enforcement, anchor-based dedup, deterministic sort)
   Diagnostics/RiskDiagnostics  (rules evaluated/findings created/deduplicated/rule failures/evidence violations)
```

- **Provider/rule-based by construction, not a growing if/else block.** Each of the twelve rules is its own small, independently constructible, independently testable class implementing one `Evaluate(RiskAnalysisContext) -> IReadOnlyList<RiskFinding>` method — `RiskRuleEngine` itself contains no rule-specific logic at all, only ordering/failure-isolation/evidence-enforcement/dedup/sort, which are the same for every rule regardless of what it checks.
- **Deterministic finding identity, not a random GUID.** `RiskFinding.ComputeId` implements exactly `risk:{RuleId}:{SourceEntityId}:{normalized-related-ids}`, where the related-id list is de-duplicated, ordinally sorted, and comma-joined before hashing into the string — so the same underlying migration risk always produces the same Id across repeated runs regardless of the order a rule happened to enumerate its related entities in, which is what makes a diff between two scan runs meaningful ("no new findings" actually means no new findings, not "the same findings got new random Ids").
- **`RiskAnalysisContext` is Risk Analysis's entire I/O surface with the outside world, and that surface is zero filesystem/registry/process/network/systemd/Docker/Kubernetes.** It is built once per run from five already-produced artifacts (`DiscoveryEntity[]`, the expanded `DependencyGraph`, `ApplicationBoundary[]`, `DependencyExpansionResult`, `GraphValidationResult`) passed into its constructor, and every index (`ById`, `Services`, `Certificates`, `BoundaryIdByEntityId`, etc.) is computed once up front so no rule ever re-scans the full entity list — this was verified structurally, not just by convention: `ServerSleuth.Analysis.csproj` has exactly one `ProjectReference` (`ServerSleuth.Core`) and zero `PackageReference`s, so there is no Windows/Linux/Infrastructure assembly, and therefore no `Microsoft.Win32.Registry`/`ServiceController`/`Process.Start`/socket/systemd/Docker/Kubernetes API, reachable from this project at all.
- **Confidence is a ceiling, not a fresh guess.** Every rule derives a finding's `Confidence` from the confidence of the specific entity/edge/evidence that justified it (e.g. `CertificateExpiryRule` uses `VeryHigh` because an expiry date is an unambiguous scanner-read fact; `ComDependencyRule` takes the lower of the COM registration's and the resolving edge's confidence) — a rule is never free to assert a confidence higher than what actually backs the finding.
- **The evidence invariant is enforced by the engine, not merely documented per rule.** `RiskRuleEngine.Analyze` drops any non-`Info` finding with zero `Evidence` records and records the drop as a diagnostic (`RiskDiagnostics.EvidenceInvariantViolations`) rather than either publishing an unproven finding or silently discarding it — a rule that ever tried to do this would have its finding disappear with a visible diagnostic trail, not fail a code review that missed it.
- **Deduplication is a narrow, explicit opt-in, not fuzzy matching.** Two findings are only ever merged when they share the exact same `Metadata["MissingBinaryEntityId"]` anchor value — the concrete case skill.md names (`MissingBinaryRule` and `ServiceDependencyRule`/`ScheduledTaskDependencyRule`/`ComDependencyRule` independently discovering the same missing executable). The merge takes the max severity, the lowest (most conservative) confidence isn't assumed — the *highest* of the contributing findings' confidences is kept since each already individually satisfied its own confidence ceiling — unions all evidence (deduped by Type/Location/Detail), and records every contributing `RuleId` in `Metadata["ContributingRules"]` so provenance is never silently lost by the merge.
- **One rule throwing never aborts the run.** `RiskRuleEngine` wraps each rule's `Evaluate` call individually; an exception is caught, recorded as a `RuleFailure` diagnostic with the exception type/message, and execution continues with the next rule in deterministic order — mirroring the same fault-isolation principle Discovery's `DiscoveryEngine` (Phase 6G) already applies to individual scanners.
- **Expected orphans are a policy, not a special case scattered through the rules.** No rule reacts to an entity's mere existence-without-a-referencer — `MissingRuntimeRule` only fires on an explicit, version-bearing `RuntimeVersion:` configuration reference (never a bare family marker, directory name, or package name); `MissingBinaryRule`/`ComDependencyRule` only fire when something in the graph actually depends on the missing binary; `CertificateExpiryRule` fires purely off the expiry window, independent of boundary membership, so an installed-but-unreferenced runtime or a certificate nobody's application uses is never itself a migration risk. This is proven by a dedicated negative-fixture suite (`NegativeRiskFixtureTests`), not just asserted in comments.
- **No score exists yet, deliberately.** `RiskSeverity` is a five-value enum a rule sets directly; there is no `RiskScore`, no weighted formula, no probability×impact calculation anywhere in `ServerSleuth.Analysis.Risk` — that is explicitly out of scope until Phase 7B (Risk Scoring), per the strict-stop condition this phase was given.

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 7A entry and `SCANNERS.md`.

## Phase 7B Addendum — Risk Scoring & Risk Aggregation (as built)

Sits immediately after Risk Analysis (7A) in the pipeline — Discovery→Correlation→Boundary→Expansion→Validation→Risk Analysis→**Risk Aggregation** — and consumes only its two outputs (`RiskAnalysisContext`, `RiskAnalysisResult`). Nothing in the 7A layer (`Rules/`, `Engine/`, `RiskAnalysisContext`) was modified to build this.

```
ServerSleuth.Analysis/Risk/ (extended — still references only ServerSleuth.Core)
   Models/
      AggregateSeverity        (None/Info/Low/Medium/High/Critical, ascending — ordinal comparison, never string sort)
      RiskSummaryBase           (abstract; common fields shared by both summary levels)
      ApplicationRiskSummary    (: RiskSummaryBase — one per ApplicationBoundary with >=1 attributed finding)
      ServerRiskSummary         (: RiskSummaryBase — covers every finding, app-scoped and server-scoped alike)
      RiskAggregationResult     (Server + Diagnostics)
   Aggregation/
      RiskSummaryCalculator     (internal — the ONE shared implementation of every metric both summary levels use)
      ApplicationRiskAggregator (internal — groups by resolved boundary Id, builds ApplicationRiskSummary[])
      ServerRiskAggregator      (internal — builds the whole-server ServerRiskSummary)
      RiskAggregator            (public entry point: Aggregate(RiskAnalysisContext, RiskAnalysisResult) -> RiskAggregationResult)
   Diagnostics/RiskAggregationDiagnostics (findings processed/dedup-upstream/app summaries created/server-level count/top risks/unresolved ownership)
```

- **Two aggregators, one calculator — never two divergent implementations of the same metric.** `ApplicationRiskAggregator` and `ServerRiskAggregator` both delegate every count/metric (`ComputeOverallSeverity`, `ComputeAffectedEntityCount`, `ComputeCategoryCounts`, `ComputeSharedDependencyCount`, `ComputeAggregateConfidence`, `ComputeTopRisks`) to the single internal `RiskSummaryCalculator` — the application level and server level can never silently disagree on how, say, "overall severity" is computed, because there is only one implementation of that computation in the codebase.
- **Severity escalation, not averaging.** `ComputeOverallSeverity` returns the highest severity present among a scope's findings (`None` only when the scope has zero findings) — a single Critical finding always makes the enclosing summary Critical, full stop; there is no numeric blending that could dilute it.
- **Boundary attribution never invents ownership.** `RiskAggregator.ResolveBoundaryId` uses a finding's own explicit `ApplicationBoundaryId` when a Phase 7A rule set one, otherwise falls back to the `SourceEntityId`'s already-computed Phase 5B boundary membership (`RiskAnalysisContext.BoundaryIdByEntityId`) — a finding that resolves to neither is correctly left server-scoped rather than force-fitted into a boundary via naming similarity or any other heuristic.
- **No finding is ever dropped.** `ServerRiskAggregator` is built over *every* input finding, not just the ones that resolved to a boundary — server-scoped findings still contribute to `ServerRiskSummary.Findings`/counts/`OverallSeverity`; only `ApplicationRiskSummary` construction is boundary-scoped.
- **Confidence aggregation is a ceiling, not a sum.** `ComputeAggregateConfidence` is the maximum single `Confidence.Value` among the scope's findings — never a sum/average that could push the aggregate's claimed confidence above what any individual piece of evidence actually supports. A dedicated test constructs 20 low-confidence findings alongside 1 high-confidence one and asserts the aggregate equals the single high value, not something diluted or inflated by the crowd.
- **Top risks are ordered by an explicit five-key cascade, never dictionary order.** Severity descending → per-finding Impact (distinct touched entities) descending → Confidence descending → `RuleId` ordinal → `Id` ordinal, capped at a fixed `TopRisksLimit = 10`. Each key in the cascade is verified by an isolated test that holds every other key equal and varies only that one.
- **Aggregation never re-deduplicates.** Phase 7A's `RiskRuleEngine` already merged same-anchor findings before Aggregation ever runs; `RiskAggregationDiagnostics.FindingsDeduplicatedUpstream` is a pass-through count for provenance, not a second, independent deduplication pass that could disagree with the first.
- **No score, no readiness claim, deliberately.** `AggregateSeverity` is a six-value enum computed directly from `RiskSeverity` values already on the input findings — there is no `RiskScore` (0-100), no weighted/probability×impact formula, and no `MigrationReady`/`MigrationBlocked`/readiness-score concept anywhere in this layer; those remain explicitly out of scope until a future Migration Assessment phase, per this phase's own strict-stop condition.
- **Purely in-memory, verified by inspection.** `ServerSleuth.Analysis.csproj` still has exactly one `ProjectReference` (`ServerSleuth.Core`) and zero `PackageReference`s after this addition — `RiskAggregator.Aggregate` only ever reads its two in-memory arguments and never mutates them (verified by a reference-equality test: every `RiskFinding` in a summary's `Findings`/`TopRisks` is the exact same object Phase 7A produced, never a copy).

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 7B entry.

## Phase 8A Addendum — Migration Assessment Model & Policy (as built)

Sits immediately after Risk Aggregation (7B) in the pipeline — ...→Risk Analysis→Risk Aggregation→**Migration Assessment** — and consumes `RiskAnalysisContext`, `RiskAnalysisResult`, and `RiskAggregationResult` only. Nothing in the 7A/7B layers was modified to build this.

```
ServerSleuth.Analysis/Migration/ (references only ServerSleuth.Core)
   Models/
      MigrationStatus            (Ready/ReadyWithConditions/NeedsRemediation/Blocked, ascending — no numeric score)
      MigrationStatusImpact      (Informational/Unclassified/Conditional/RemediationRequired/Blocking, ascending)
      MigrationIssue             (one per contributing RiskFinding — full traceability + PolicyDecisionReason)
      MigrationDependency        (+ MigrationDependencyType, MigrationVerificationPhase — separate from risk)
      MigrationAssessmentBase / ApplicationMigrationAssessment / ServerMigrationAssessment / MigrationAssessmentSummary
   Assessment/
      MigrationPolicy            (internal — the explicit RuleId-driven Critical-does-not-mean-Blocked table)
      MigrationPolicyEvaluator   (internal — the ONE place a MigrationIssue is constructed)
      MigrationAssessmentCalculator (internal — shared metric/status implementation)
      MigrationAssessmentEngine  (public entry point: Assess(context, riskResult, aggregation) -> MigrationAssessmentSummary)
   Diagnostics/MigrationDiagnostics (findings evaluated, issues/dependencies created, unclassified-impact records)
```

- **RuleId-primary, Severity-secondary policy.** `MigrationPolicy.Classify` keys on `RuleId` first — a Critical `RiskSeverity` never automatically means `Blocking` (an expired-Critical certificate, RR5, is `RemediationRequired`); Severity only refines the two rules whose severity genuinely varies per-finding (RR2, RR5).
- **Dependencies modeled separately from risk.** A `MigrationDependency` can exist with `RelatedRiskFindingId == null` — discovered from `ExternalDependency` entities, `BoundaryDiagnostics.SharedBinaries`, and Certificate/MissingRuntime findings, never fabricated for every certificate/runtime on the box.
- **Shared-infrastructure reuse, not reinvention.** Reuses `RiskAggregator.ResolveAffectedBoundaryIds` directly for both server- and application-level attribution, so the Phase 7B hardening's fan-out behavior applies here automatically.

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 8A entry.

## Phase 8B Addendum — Migration Actions & Verification Planning (as built)

Sits immediately after Migration Assessment (8A) — ...→Migration Assessment→**Migration Planning** — and consumes only `MigrationAssessmentSummary`. Nothing in the 7A/7B/8A layers was modified to build this.

```
ServerSleuth.Analysis/Migration/ (extended — still references only ServerSleuth.Core)
   Actions/
      MigrationActionType        (17-value closed taxonomy — shared by MigrationAction.ActionType AND MigrationVerificationCheck.CheckType)
      MigrationActionPriority    (Critical/High/Medium/Low/Informational — 1:1 from the originating issue's RiskSeverity)
      MigrationAction            (declarative — WHAT must be addressed, never HOW; Evidence/Rationale traced to its Issue)
      MigrationActionDiagnostics
      MigrationActionPlanner     (public: Plan(ServerMigrationAssessment) -> (Actions, Diagnostics))
   Verification/
      MigrationVerificationCheck (declarative checklist item — reuses Migration.Models.MigrationVerificationPhase)
      MigrationVerificationDiagnostics
      MigrationVerificationPlanner (public: Plan(ServerMigrationAssessment, Actions) -> (PreChecks, PostChecks, Diagnostics))
   Planning/
      MigrationPlan               (Assessment + Actions + Dependencies + PreMigrationChecks + PostMigrationChecks + Diagnostics)
      MigrationPlanDiagnostics
      MigrationPlanEngine         (public entry point: Plan(MigrationAssessmentSummary) -> MigrationPlan)
```

- **One taxonomy, not two.** `MigrationActionType` describes both "the kind of action" and "the kind of check" — skill.md §5's own example list already names several `Verify*` entries as action types, and §9-13 ask for the identical vocabulary on checks. A second, near-duplicate `MigrationCheckType` enum was deliberately not created.
- **Deduplication is mostly inherited, not reinvented.** Phase 7A's `MissingBinaryEntityId` merge anchor already collapses cross-rule findings about the same missing binary into one `RiskFinding`/`MigrationIssue`, and Phase 8A already fans a shared dependency's `AffectedBoundaryIds` out onto one Issue rather than duplicating it per boundary. `MigrationActionPlanner` therefore only needs to fold `MigrationDependency` records into an existing action via `RelatedRiskFindingId` (indexed once, O(D), never O(Issues × Dependencies)) — it does not need its own fuzzy-matching merge pass.
- **Dependencies without risk are never dropped.** A `MigrationDependency` with `RelatedRiskFindingId == null`, or whose Issue produced no action (Informational/Unclassified impact), still receives its own standalone PostMigration `MigrationVerificationCheck` from `MigrationVerificationPlanner` — counted separately in `MigrationVerificationDiagnostics.OrphanDependencyChecksCreated` so it's visible this happened on purpose.
- **Boundary-kind refinement from Id prefix, not raw entities.** Since this layer only ever sees `MigrationAssessmentSummary` (not raw `DiscoveryEntity`s), a `PrepareMissingBinary` action's post-check `CheckType` is refined by parsing its `AffectedBoundaryIds`' own Id prefix (`boundary:service:`/`boundary:scheduledtask:`/`boundary:iis-application:`, the same convention every scanner already uses to build boundary Ids) rather than requiring a second discovery pass.
- **No action ever describes execution.** `MigrationActionType`'s entire taxonomy is `Prepare*`/`Verify*`/`Review*`/`Document*` — there is structurally no "Execute"/"Run" member, and no action/check `Description` is ever built from a shell command template.
- **Purely in-memory, verified by inspection.** `ServerSleuth.Analysis.csproj` still has exactly one `ProjectReference` (`ServerSleuth.Core`) and zero `PackageReference`s after this addition — `MigrationPlanEngine.Plan` only ever reads its one in-memory argument and never mutates it (verified by an Id-sequence before/after snapshot test).

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 8B entry.

## Phase 8C Addendum — Server/Application Migration Assessment Consolidation (as built)

Sits immediately after Migration Planning (8B) — ...→Migration Assessment→Migration Planning→**Consolidation** — and consumes `RiskAnalysisContext`, `RiskAggregationResult`, `MigrationAssessmentSummary`, `MigrationPlan`, and an optional `AggregateDiscoveryResult`. Nothing in the 5B/5C/5D/7A/7B/8A/8B layers was modified to build this.

```
ServerSleuth.Analysis/Migration/Consolidation/ (references only ServerSleuth.Core)
   AssessmentCoverage          (Unknown/Limited/Partial/Complete — derived from AggregateDiscoveryResult only)
   CoverageWarning             (ScannerId/ScannerStatus/Reason/AffectedPlatform/Evidence — never fabricated)
   ServerMigrationSummary      (whole-server rollup — every field copied verbatim or a plain count)
   ApplicationMigrationSummary (wraps Phase 8A's ApplicationMigrationAssessment + RiskSeverity + Actions/Checks)
   MigrationDependencyGroup    (Type + Dependencies — existing MigrationDependencyType, no new identity scheme)
   ConsolidationDiagnostics
   ServerMigrationAssessmentReport   (the final Server -> Application -> Dependency -> Issue -> Action -> Verification view)
   ServerMigrationAssessmentReportEngine  (public entry point: Build(context, aggregation, assessment, plan, discovery?) -> report)
```

- **Composition, not a second analysis engine (skill.md §2 — the phase's own central architectural constraint).** Every status/severity/action/check value is copied verbatim from an already-produced Phase 7B/8A/8B artifact. The engine's own logic is limited to: joining `ApplicationMigrationAssessment` (8A) with `ApplicationRiskSummary` (7B) by boundary Id (both are built from the same `aggregation.Server.ApplicationSummaries` iteration, so the join always succeeds); indexing `MigrationPlan`'s flat Action/Check lists by affected boundary Id once (O(Actions+Checks), not O(Applications×Actions)); grouping dependencies by their existing `MigrationDependencyType`; and deriving `AssessmentCoverage` from scanner-status information. No `MigrationPolicy`, `MigrationActionPlanner`, or `GraphValidator` logic is re-implemented or re-invoked here.
- **Coverage and MigrationStatus are computed from disjoint inputs, by design (skill.md §12).** `AssessmentCoverage` reads only `AggregateDiscoveryResult`; `ServerMigrationSummary.OverallMigrationStatus` is copied verbatim from `MigrationAssessmentSummary.Server.OverallStatus`. There is no code path connecting the two — a server can legitimately be `Ready` with `Limited` coverage, and the existing Phase 8A `MigrationPolicy` remains the sole authority for whether missing evidence is itself a migration blocker.
- **Shared infrastructure and server-level issues are filters, not new data.** `SharedInfrastructure` = dependencies with >1 `AffectedBoundaryIds`; `ServerLevelIssues` = issues with 0 `AffectedBoundaryIds`. Both reference the exact same Phase 8A object instances that `MigrationAssessmentSummary.Server` already carries.
- **Graph validation findings pass through unmodified.** `GraphValidationErrors` filters `RiskAnalysisContext.Validation.Findings` to Error severity — `GraphValidator` (Phase 5D) is never re-run, and the existing RR12-GraphIntegrity policy (Phase 8A, always Blocking) remains the sole Blocked/Ready authority for these findings; this list exists purely for visibility in the consolidated view.

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 8C entry.

## Phase 9A Addendum — Reporting Foundation & JSON Renderer (as built)

New top-level project, `ServerSleuth.Reporting` (plus `ServerSleuth.Reporting.Tests`) — the first project outside `ServerSleuth.Analysis`/`ServerSleuth.Core` in this pipeline segment. Sits strictly downstream of Phase 8C: ...→Consolidation→**Reporting**, consuming only `ServerMigrationAssessmentReport`. Nothing in the 5B-8C layers was modified to build this.

```
ServerSleuth.Reporting/ (references only ServerSleuth.Analysis, transitively ServerSleuth.Core)
   IReportRenderer            (Format + Render(ServerMigrationAssessmentReport) -> ReportRenderResult)
   ReportFormat                (Json — the only member; no unused HTML/PDF placeholder)
   ReportRenderResult          (Format/Content/Encoding — in-memory only, no file/HTTP/email output)
   Json/
      JsonReportRenderer       (System.Text.Json + JsonStringEnumConverter + JavaScriptEncoder.Create(UnicodeRanges.All))
      Dto/
         ServerReportDto, ServerSummaryDto, ApplicationDto, IssueDto, DependencyDto,
         DependencyGroupDto, ActionDto, CheckDto, EvidenceDto, ConfidenceDto,
         CoverageWarningDto, GraphValidationFindingDto, DiagnosticsDto
         ReportDtoMapper       (internal — the ONE pure, 1:1 ServerMigrationAssessmentReport -> ServerReportDto mapping)
```

- **A dedicated DTO tree, not direct domain-model serialization, IS the secret-safety boundary (skill.md §6).** `JsonReportRenderer` never calls `JsonSerializer.Serialize` on `ServerMigrationAssessmentReport`/`MigrationIssue`/etc. directly — it always goes through `ReportDtoMapper.ToDto()` first. Because each DTO's property list is fixed and explicitly declared, it structurally cannot expose a field the mapper didn't deliberately copy — a future domain-model field addition (e.g. carelessly threading entity `Metadata` through a new Phase 8-series model) would need a corresponding, reviewable DTO+mapper change before it could reach JSON, rather than silently appearing via reflection/dynamic serialization of the domain type.
- **This is a second, independent safety layer on top of what already exists.** `ServerSleuth.Core.Models.Configuration` never carries raw file content (Phase 4E-1/6E), and Phase 8A/8B/8C never carry entity `Metadata` forward into any Migration-layer model at all — so in practice no secret-shaped value can reach Reporting through the current model. The DTO boundary exists so that guarantee doesn't depend SOLELY on every upstream phase staying disciplined forever; it's enforced structurally at the one place all output funnels through.
- **Determinism (§8) is inherited, not re-implemented.** `ReportDtoMapper` never sorts/groups/deduplicates a collection — every `Select().ToList()` walks a list Phase 8C already produced in deterministic ordinal order. The mapper itself is the only place order could be broken (e.g. by iterating a `Dictionary`), and it doesn't.
- **Unicode readability over the default escaping (§9).** `System.Text.Json`'s default encoder escapes all non-ASCII as `\uXXXX` — valid but unreadable for a report a human opens directly. `JavaScriptEncoder.Create(UnicodeRanges.All)` keeps literal characters (e.g. Traditional Chinese) while the JSON writer still escapes all structurally-required characters (quotes, backslashes, control characters) regardless of encoder choice.
- **Errors are not caught (§11).** `JsonReportRenderer.Render` has no try/catch — this is deliberate: a caught-and-rethrown or caught-and-wrapped exception is easy to accidentally turn into a swallowed one later; letting `System.Text.Json` exceptions propagate unmodified is both simpler and strictly safer against that mistake.

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 9A entry.

## Phase 9B Addendum — Self-Contained HTML Migration Report (as built)

Extends `ServerSleuth.Reporting` in place — no new project, no second reporting architecture. Sits beside `Json/` as a sibling implementation of the same `IReportRenderer` interface.

```
ServerSleuth.Reporting/Html/ (still references only ServerSleuth.Analysis, transitively ServerSleuth.Core)
   HtmlReportRenderer     (public: Format => ReportFormat.Html; Render(report) -> ReportRenderResult)
   HtmlDocumentStyles     (const string Css — the report's one inline stylesheet)
   CssClassName           (internal — PascalCase status/severity/coverage string -> kebab-case CSS class, pure syntax)
```

- **One DTO tree, two renderers.** `HtmlReportRenderer.Render` calls the same internal `ReportDtoMapper.ToDto()` `JsonReportRenderer` calls — `Json/Dto` was never JSON-specific, just internal to the assembly. This is why HTML inherits the exact same secret-safety/raw-configuration-safety guarantee JSON already has, with zero new redaction logic: there was never a second place secrets could leak from.
- **Self-contained by construction, not by convention.** There is no code path anywhere in `HtmlReportRenderer` that writes a `<link>`, `<script src>`, `<img src="http...">`, or any other URL-bearing tag — the renderer's own vocabulary of HTML fragments (built via `StringBuilder.Append` calls in fixed private methods) simply doesn't include one. Offline operation isn't a policy the renderer follows; it's a capability the renderer's code doesn't have.
- **`<details>`/`<summary>` over JavaScript.** Per-application sections collapse/expand using native HTML, which needs zero script. This is stronger than "the report is readable without JavaScript" (§20's stated minimum) — it means there is no JavaScript-dependent behavior AT ALL to distinguish "readable" from "fully functional."
- **`HtmlEncoder.Create(UnicodeRanges.All)`, matching the JSON renderer's `JavaScriptEncoder` choice.** `System.Net.WebUtility.HtmlEncode` (the more commonly reached-for BCL API) numeric-entity-escapes Latin-1 Supplement characters while leaving CJK alone — inconsistent and, for accented Western text, less readable than necessary. `System.Text.Encodings.Web.HtmlEncoder` with `UnicodeRanges.All` keeps all non-ASCII literal while still always escaping the five HTML-structural characters regardless of range configuration (a property of `HtmlEncoder` itself, not something the range parameter can override) — verified with adversarial script/attribute-injection payloads specifically to confirm that guarantee holds under this configuration.
- **CSS classes are a syntactic transform of an existing value, never a second classification.** `CssClassName.From("NeedsRemediation")` → `"needs-remediation"` — pure regex-based PascalCase→kebab-case, applied identically regardless of which of the ~20 status/severity/coverage/impact/priority enum values it receives. There is no switch statement mapping values to CSS classes anywhere; if there were, it would be a second place a status could be silently reinterpreted.
- **Action grouping reads structured fields, never Description/Rationale text.** Pre-Migration/Post-Migration/Review-Documentation bucketing checks `ActionType`'s own string prefix (`Review`/`Document`) and `Phase` — both fields `MigrationActionPlanner` (Phase 8B) already populated deliberately, not text the renderer would need to parse or guess at.
- **Determinism is opt-in-timestamp, not zero-configuration-only.** `HtmlReportRenderer(DateTimeOffset? generatedAt = null)` lets a caller choose to show a generation timestamp without making that the default, and without making the DEFAULT renderer non-deterministic to support the option — the two concerns (reproducibility, timestamp display) are cleanly separated by one constructor parameter.

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 9B entry.

## Phase 9C Addendum — Report Artifact & Local Export (as built)

Extends `ServerSleuth.Reporting` with the first filesystem-writing component in the whole Reporting layer. Sits strictly downstream of both existing renderers.

```
ServerSleuth.Reporting/Export/ (still references only ServerSleuth.Analysis, transitively ServerSleuth.Core)
   ReportArtifact              (FileName/Format/Content/Encoding/ContentLength — immutable)
   ReportBundle                (Json + Html artifacts, always from the same report instance)
   ReportArtifactFactory       (wraps JsonReportRenderer/HtmlReportRenderer output; no analysis)
   ReportOverwritePolicy       (FailIfExists default, Overwrite)
   ReportExportResult / ReportBundleExportResult   (result model — expected failures never thrown)
   ReportManifest / ReportManifestEntry            (opt-in, safe-metadata-only, SHA-256)
   ReportFileNameValidator     (internal — platform-independent path/traversal safety check)
   IReportExporter
   LocalFileReportExporter     (the only concrete exporter)
```

- **Filesystem access, but still platform-neutral.** `LocalFileReportExporter` is the first type in `ServerSleuth.Reporting` to touch `System.IO` at all — and it uses only the BCL (`Directory`, `FileStream`, `File`), against exactly the directory the caller passed in. No project reference was added; `dotnet list reference` after this phase still shows only `ServerSleuth.Analysis`.
- **Atomic write is same-directory-temp-file-then-move, documented with its one real limitation.** The temp file lives beside the final file specifically so the promoting `File.Move` is a same-filesystem rename (atomic on both Windows and Linux) rather than a cross-volume copy (never atomic). The doc comment on `LocalFileReportExporter` states this limitation explicitly rather than implying universal atomicity.
- **File-name safety is a platform-independent blacklist, not `Path`'s own (platform-dependent) validation helpers.** `Path.GetInvalidFileNameChars()` returns a different set on Windows vs. Linux (only NUL on Linux) — relying on it alone would let `<script>` through unchecked on a Linux host. `ReportFileNameValidator` checks an explicit, fixed character/pattern set instead, so a file name is accepted or rejected identically regardless of the host OS running the exporter.
- **`ReportBundle` can only ever be internally consistent.** `ReportArtifactFactory.CreateBundle` is the sole constructor of a `ReportBundle`, and it takes one `ServerMigrationAssessmentReport` argument, rendering both formats from it in the same call — there is no API shape that could produce a bundle whose two artifacts describe different assessments.
- **Manifest hashing uses the literal write buffer, not a second read-back.** `BuildManifestEntry` computes SHA-256 over the same `byte[]` that gets passed to `FileStream.Write` — the integrity guarantee (`SHA256(read(file)) == manifest.Sha256`) holds by construction rather than by two independent computations happening to agree.

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 9C entry.

## Phase 10A Addendum — CLI Foundation (as built)

New top-level project, `ServerSleuth.Cli` (the `serversleuth` executable) plus `ServerSleuth.Cli.Tests` — the composition root that finally ties every prior phase together into one runnable tool.

```
ServerSleuth.Cli/ (multi-targeted net8.0-windows;net8.0 — see its own .csproj comment)
   Program.cs              (Ctrl+C -> CancellationTokenSource, constructs CliApplication, returns exit code)
   CliApplication           (testable core: --help/--version/scan dispatch, top-level error handling)
   Composition/
      CompositionRoot        (AddLogging -> AddServerSleuthInfrastructure -> platform -> AddServerSleuthDiscoveryEngine)
   Commands/
      ScanCommand             (orchestrates Pipeline + Output + Export for `scan`)
   Options/
      ScanOptions, ReportFormatOption, ScanOptionsParser, CliArgumentException
   Pipeline/
      ScanPipelineRunner      (Discovery -> Correlation -> ... -> ServerMigrationAssessmentReport)
      ReportExportRunner      (ReportArtifactFactory -> IReportExporter, per requested format)
   Output/
      IConsoleWriter, TextWriterConsoleWriter, HelpText, ScanProgressReporter, VersionInfo
   ExitCodes/
      CliExitCode
```

- **Multi-targeting is the load-bearing decision that makes the whole project possible.** A single `net8.0` project cannot restore a `ProjectReference` to `ServerSleuth.Windows` (`net8.0-windows`-only) at all — NuGet's NU1201 compatibility check rejects it regardless of `EnableWindowsTargeting` (that property only helps with Windows Desktop SDK *package* references, not an incompatible project reference). `<TargetFrameworks>net8.0-windows;net8.0</TargetFrameworks>` with a `SERVERSLEUTH_WINDOWS`-gated `#if` around the `AddServerSleuthWindows()` call is the standard, correct pattern: the `net8.0-windows` build is what actually ships on Windows (with Windows discovery compiled in), the plain `net8.0` build is what ships on Linux. The runtime OS check (`OperatingSystem.IsWindows()`) still governs whether the call actually executes — the TFM split only controls whether it's even compiled in.
- **`DiscoveryEngine` remains exactly as built in Phase 6G — zero platform awareness added.** All platform selection lives in `CompositionRoot`, which decides which `IDiscoveryScanner`s exist in the container at all; `DiscoveryEngine` still just iterates `IDiscoveryScannerRegistry.Scanners` in registry order, unaware of and indifferent to which OS registered them.
- **The pipeline wiring is deliberately boring.** `ScanPipelineRunner.Analyze` is a straight-line sequence of calls into `CorrelationEngine`/`ApplicationBoundaryEngine`/`DependencyExpansionEngine`/`GraphValidator`/`RiskAnalysisContext`/`RiskRuleEngine`/`RiskAggregator`/`MigrationAssessmentEngine`/`MigrationPlanEngine`/`ServerMigrationAssessmentReportEngine` — the exact same shape as `RiskPipeline`/`TestPipeline`, the test-only helpers every prior phase's own fixture tests already used. This is intentional: the CLI is the first PRODUCTION code to run that exact sequence, and matching the test helpers' own shape as closely as possible is what makes "no reimplementation" independently verifiable by inspection.
- **Report writing has exactly one path.** `ReportExportRunner` is the only code in the CLI that touches `IReportExporter`; there is no `File.WriteAllText`/`StreamWriter` anywhere else in `ServerSleuth.Cli`. `--format` selects WHICH artifact(s) get exported, never how they're rendered (`ReportArtifactFactory.CreateBundle` always renders both, cheaply, in memory — only the requested one(s) reach disk).
- **Exit codes are this phase's own invention, explicitly documented as such.** A grep for "exit code" across skill.md/ARCHITECTURE.md/IMPLEMENTATION_PLAN.md/PROGRESS.md returned zero matches before this phase — there was no existing spec to violate or conform to, so `CliExitCode`'s doc comment states plainly that this is Phase 10A's own definition, including the one addition (`5 = Cancelled`) beyond skill.md §15's stated "at minimum" four.
- **`ScanProgressReporter` and `CliExitCode`'s `PartialDiscovery` trigger set both reuse Phase 8C's own `AssessmentCoverage` classification (PartiallySupported/AccessDenied/Failed = degraded; NotApplicable/NotInstalled = neutral)** rather than inventing a third, CLI-specific notion of "how complete was this scan" — one classification, referenced from two places, instead of two that could drift apart.

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 10A entry.

## Phase 10A-H Addendum — Real-Scale Analysis Performance Investigation (as built)

Diagnosis-only phase — no production code changed anywhere in the solution. Adds one new location: `test/ServerSleuth.Analysis.Tests/Diagnostics/`.

```
ServerSleuth.Analysis.Tests/Diagnostics/ (test-only, no production reference)
   StageTiming                (safe-only record: name/duration/counts, never raw content)
   StageMeasurement            (Stopwatch + optional diagnostic-only Task.Wait(timeout) wrapper)
   RealisticScaleFixtureBuilder (COM-heavy synthetic entity generator, modeled on the real scan)
   PipelineTimingInvestigationTests (Theory over 1,000/10,000/35,000 entities)
```

- **The investigation confirmed a real, specific, previously-undiagnosed root cause.** `ServerSleuth.Core.Graph.DependencyGraph` stores edges in a plain `List<DependencyEdge>` and implements `AddEdge` via `_edges.FindIndex(...)` (a full linear scan on every insertion, to find a mergeable duplicate) and `EdgesFrom`/`EdgesTo` via `_edges.Where(...)` (a full linear scan per call) — none of the three is indexed. This was invisible in every prior phase's own performance tests because those all used either small (17-entity ERP-scale) or artificially flat/disconnected (10k-entity, near-zero-edge) synthetic fixtures — neither shape exercises the O(edge-count) cost of these operations at any meaningful scale.
- **The specific stage that hangs is `DependencyExpansionEngine.BuildComAssociations`**, which calls `graph.EdgesFrom(com.Id)` inside a `foreach (boundary) foreach (com)` nested loop — turning an already-O(E) primitive into an O(Boundaries × ComComponents × Edges) cost. This is exactly why Expansion's measured growth (271.5ms at 1,000 entities, still not complete after 60 seconds at 10,000) is measurably steeper than Correlation's/Boundary's own already-super-linear (≈O(N^1.8-2.1)) growth, which comes purely from `DependencyGraph`'s own O(E) primitives being called once per entity elsewhere in the pipeline (15+ such call sites found across `GraphValidator`, `DependencyExpansionEngine`, `ApplicationBoundaryEngine`, and four Risk rules).
- **The investigation harness is deliberately structured to never alter behavior.** `StageMeasurement.Measure` calls the exact existing method with the exact existing arguments; the only "new" code path is the `Stopwatch`/`Task.Wait` wrapper around it. A stage that exceeds the 60-second diagnostic cap is simply abandoned as an orphaned background-thread `Task` (acceptable for a one-off diagnostic test process, explicitly not something that would ever ship) — no thread-abort, no process-kill logic, and specifically never anything that could reach a scanned server process.
- **No optimization was implemented.** The recommended fix (replace `DependencyGraph`'s linear list with an indexed structure, e.g. `Dictionary<string, List<DependencyEdge>>` by source/target Id) and the secondary fix (`BuildComAssociations` pre-indexing COM→target edges once rather than re-scanning per boundary) are both documented in `PROGRESS.md` as targets for a future phase, per this phase's own strict-stop instruction.

Full detail is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 10A-H entry.

## Phase 10A-I Addendum — Dependency Graph Performance Optimization (as built)

Implements Phase 10A-H's own recommendation. Two files changed: `src/ServerSleuth.Core/Graph/DependencyGraph.cs` and `src/ServerSleuth.Analysis/Correlation/Expansion/DependencyExpansionEngine.cs` (`BuildComAssociations` only). No other production file in the solution was touched.

```
DependencyGraph (Core.Graph)
   _nodes                  Dictionary<string, DiscoveryEntity>      — unchanged
   _edges                  List<DependencyEdge>                     — unchanged, still authoritative/ordered
   _edgeIndicesBySource     Dictionary<string, List<int>>            — NEW, private, indices into _edges
   _edgeIndicesByTarget     Dictionary<string, List<int>>            — NEW, private, indices into _edges
```

- **The index stores `int` positions into `_edges`, not a second `DependencyEdge` reference — a deliberate deviation from a literal `Dictionary<string, List<DependencyEdge>>` reading of the task.** Storing indices means a duplicate-edge merge (`AddEdge`'s existing evidence-union/highest-confidence-wins behavior) only ever needs one write — `_edges[existingIndex] = existing with { ... }` — because both index dictionaries still point at the same, now-updated slot. A design that stored `DependencyEdge` values directly in the indexes would need to also locate and replace the same logical edge inside the target-side index on every merge (an O(in-degree) scan with no cheap shortcut, since the merge is discovered via the source-side index only), which is unnecessary complexity this phase's own "conceptually equivalent" framing doesn't require.
- **`AddEdge`'s duplicate-merge check (`FindExistingEdgeIndex`) is now O(out-degree of the edge's source entity)** — it only scans `_edgeIndicesBySource[edge.SourceEntityId]`, never the full `_edges` list. A genuinely new edge appends to `_edges` and both indexes in O(1) amortized time.
- **`EdgesFrom`/`EdgesTo` are now O(out-degree)/O(in-degree)** — a dictionary lookup plus a `Select` projection over that entity's own index list, versus the previous O(total edge count) `_edges.Where(...)` scan. Public signatures are byte-for-byte unchanged.
- **Ordering is preserved exactly.** Each index list is built by appending in the same order edges are inserted into `_edges`, so `EdgesFrom`/`EdgesTo` still return "canonical insertion order, filtered by entity Id" — identical to the pre-optimization `_edges.Where(...)` behavior, and never dependent on `Dictionary`/`HashSet` enumeration order. Verified with a new `[Theory]` determinism test building the same edges via two different insertion orders.
- **Graph cloning needed zero code changes.** `DependencyExpansionEngine.CloneGraph` (the only clone path in the codebase) already builds a fresh `new DependencyGraph()` and repopulates it via the public `AddNode`/`AddEdge` API — since every `DependencyGraph` instance owns its own private index dictionaries with no static/shared state, clone/source index independence is structural by construction, not something `CloneGraph` has to arrange. Verified anyway with two explicit mutation-independence tests (both directions) at the `DependencyExpansionEngine.Expand` public entry point.
- **`BuildComAssociations` was restructured around one pre-computed, once-per-expansion index rather than a per-boundary re-scan of every COM component.** `comsByReferencedBinary` (binary Id → COMs whose References edge targets it, built in one O(ComComponents) pass using the now-indexed `EdgesFrom`) replaces the old `foreach (boundary) { foreach (ALL com) { graph.EdgesFrom(com.Id)... } }` pattern. Per boundary, a small `candidateComIds` set is derived from the boundary's own members plus every binary any member IMPORTs — exactly the two target shapes Tier 1 (direct member match, `Confidence.High()`) and Tier 2 (member IMPORTS the COM's target, `Confidence.Medium()`) already check — and only those candidates are evaluated, in `context.ComComponents`' original relative order (`comOrder`), running the *unmodified* per-com, per-edge, break-on-first-match logic. No new association path was introduced; the 200-unrelated-COM negative fixture (and a new 51-boundary/3,000-COM variant) both still produce zero false associations.
- **Real-machine validation went further than the synthetic re-run alone.** A `serversleuth.exe scan` run against the same dev host that previously hung (34,925 entities) now progresses past Expansion and reaches Risk Analysis, where it hits a separate, pre-existing bug in `RiskRuleEngine.Merge` (Phase 7A) — `Metadata["MissingBinaryEntityId"]` is read unconditionally under the assumption that any `Deduplicate` group with more than one member shares that anchor key, which is false for a `"solo:{f.Id}"` group formed by an `Id` collision between two findings from rules that never set that key. This bug was structurally unreachable before this phase (Expansion always hung first at real-machine scale) and was deliberately left unfixed here, per this phase's own DependencyGraph-and-COM-lookup-only scope.
- **No thread safety was added.** `DependencyGraph` was not, and is still not, used as a thread-safe type anywhere in the codebase (every construction/mutation happens from a single thread/async continuation) — no lock, no `ConcurrentDictionary`, nothing changed about its threading contract.

Full detail — including the per-scale synthetic timing table, the memory-trade-off estimate, and the real-machine run's exact console output — is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 10A-I entry.

## Phase 10A-J Addendum — RiskRuleEngine.Merge Real-Scale Bug Fix (as built)

Fixes the exact `KeyNotFoundException` Phase 10A-I's real-machine run surfaced. One production file changed: `src/ServerSleuth.Analysis/Risk/Engine/RiskRuleEngine.cs` (`Merge`, one statement). No other production file in the solution was touched.

- **The root cause is a genuine "duplicate finding identity" case, traced to the actual implementation, not assumed.** `MissingDependencyRule` (RR1) emits one `RiskFinding` per `GraphValidator` `"UnresolvedBinary"` validation finding (`MissingDependencyRule.cs:38`); `GraphValidator.ValidateUnresolvedDependencies` (`GraphValidator.cs:507-515`) emits one such validation finding **per unresolved import name** on a `Dll` node, not one per DLL. A real DLL commonly imports several system/vendor libraries never discovered as entities — the worst case found on this dev host was 17 unresolved imports on one binary — so `MissingDependencyRule` produces that many `RiskFinding` objects, all sharing the same `SourceEntityId`. `RiskFinding.Id` (`RiskFinding.cs:46-53`) is computed as `risk:{RuleId}:{SourceEntityId}:{normalized-related-ids}`, with `RelatedEntityIds` always empty for RR1 — so every one of those emissions computes the exact same deterministic Id. This shape is unreachable at every prior phase's small/ERP-scale fixture (no binary there ever had more than one unresolved import), which is exactly why it was never caught before a dense, real ~34,900-entity machine scan reached it for the first time.
- **`RiskRuleEngine.Deduplicate`'s own grouping already correctly distinguishes the two cases this method is meant to handle — the bug was entirely in `Merge` assuming only one of them could ever produce a multi-member group.** `Deduplicate` groups findings by `f.Metadata.TryGetValue("MissingBinaryEntityId", ...) ? $"anchor:{anchor}" : $"solo:{f.Id}"`. An `"anchor:"` group is the one case skill.md (Phase 7A) §25 actually specifies: an explicit, opt-in cross-rule merge (`MissingBinaryRule`/`ServiceDependencyRule`/`ScheduledTaskDependencyRule`/`ComDependencyRule` all sharing a `MissingBinaryEntityId` value). A `"solo:{f.Id}"` group with more than one member is a DIFFERENT, previously-unanticipated case: the same rule accidentally emitting the exact same `Id` more than once, with no opt-in metadata at all. Both group shapes were routed to the same `Merge` method, but `Merge`'s first line unconditionally indexed `primary.Metadata["MissingBinaryEntityId"]` — correct for the first shape (every member has that key, by construction of the grouping key), never valid for the second (by definition, no member has it).
- **The fix generalizes `Merge`'s anchor computation to a value every colliding member already agrees on, rather than special-casing the two group shapes.** `anchor = primary.Metadata.TryGetValue("MissingBinaryEntityId", out var explicitAnchor) ? explicitAnchor : primary.SourceEntityId`. For an `"anchor:"` group this is byte-for-byte the previous behavior. For a `"solo:"` collision, every member shares one `SourceEntityId` by construction (that identity is what made their Ids collide in the first place) — so the fallback is never a guess, and every other part of `Merge` (evidence union, max severity, highest confidence, `ContributingRules` metadata, the final `"merged:"`-prefixed Id) was already anchor-value-agnostic and required no change.
- **`MissingDependencyRule`/`GraphValidator` were deliberately not modified.** Aggregating a DLL's multiple unresolved-import findings at the rule level (or discriminating their `Id` by import name) would also "fix" the reachable state, but would change what `MissingDependencyRule` emits — an explicit RiskRule-semantics change this phase's own scope forbids. Fixing `Merge` to correctly and gracefully absorb a same-Id collision, using the exact same evidence-preserving merge machinery already trusted for the anchor case, is the smaller, more defensible change: a rule that happens to re-emit a finding under its own already-deterministic Id is now always safely deduplicated by the engine, regardless of which specific rule does it or why.
- **Real-machine validation went all the way to a complete scan.** The same dev host that previously hung at Expansion (Phase 10A-H) and then crashed at Risk Analysis (Phase 10A-I) now completes Discovery → Correlation → Boundary → Expansion → Validation → Risk → Aggregation → Migration Assessment → Migration Planning → Consolidation → Reporting → Export in one run, producing both `report.json` and `report.html`. No further blocker was found.

Full detail — including the exact reproduced exception/stack trace, the real-machine console output, and the full regression numbers — is in `PROGRESS.md`'s Phase 10A-J entry.

## Phase 10B Addendum — CLI Production Hardening (as built)

Hardens the existing CLI foundation (Phase 10A) without touching any pipeline stage. Files changed: `src/ServerSleuth.Cli/Options/ScanOptions.cs`, `ScanOptionsParser.cs`, `src/ServerSleuth.Cli/Output/HelpText.cs`, `ScanProgressReporter.cs`, `src/ServerSleuth.Cli/Commands/ScanCommand.cs`, plus new/extended test files under `test/ServerSleuth.Cli.Tests/`. No file outside `ServerSleuth.Cli`/`ServerSleuth.Cli.Tests` was touched.

- **`--verbose` was the one option this phase judged both architecturally consistent and reliably testable, per its own §3 instruction to not blindly implement every suggested option.** It surfaces exactly the two things skill.md (Phase 10B) §5-6 actually ask for — clear per-stage progress and real per-scanner status/counts — using only data the pipeline already computes (`AggregateDiscoveryResult.ScannerResults`) and a plain `System.Diagnostics.Stopwatch` measured entirely inside `ScanCommand` (a CLI-layer concern; no domain model, no `DiscoveryResult`/`ScannerStatus` type, was touched to support it).
- **Color/`--no-color` was deliberately NOT implemented.** The existing `ScanProgressReporter` has never emitted ANSI color, and every status/count it prints is already unambiguous as plain text — adding conditional color solely because §14 permits it (it is explicitly optional: "If color is implemented...") would introduce environment-detection logic (`Console.IsOutputRedirected`) that is awkward to verify in a headless CI test runner, for zero required behavior change. This is recorded as a Decisions Log entry below rather than left as an unexplained gap.
